A Reader for Teachers and Parents

Talking with Teens

Seven books. Six modules. One question worth asking your way through. A reader for the part of teaching, and the part of parenting, that nobody hands you a manual for.

7 Books 6 Modules ~13 Hours

Adolescence is the part of teaching nobody gives you a manual for. The training shows up for math instruction, for classroom management, for IEP paperwork. The actual experience of being in a room with twenty-five middle schoolers, half of whom are processing something they can't name, isn't on any syllabus. This is a reader for that part.

The structure is six modules. Each one pairs a short stack of readings with a verified author interview (podcast or video), walks you through the material using my district's Chunk-Chew-Check format, and asks for a small concrete artifact at the end. About two hours of work per module. Read them in order, or jump to the one your week needs.

Chunk-Chew-Check is the format my colleagues and I use with eighth graders, and it works on adult readers for the same reason it works on kids: it slows the input down enough that something actually gets metabolized. Chunk is the input (read this, watch this, listen to this). Chew is the processing move (what to look for, what to mark). Check is the artifact (a sentence to write, a script to draft, a paragraph for a substitute). The Check is the part that keeps this from being a podcast queue.

§ About

Where this came from

This is a reader I built for myself first, and then for the colleagues and parents who kept asking what I was reading. I teach 8th grade Social Studies at Alderwood Middle School in the Edmonds School District, and I've spent the last several years collecting the books, podcasts, and interviews that actually changed how I show up for thirteen-year-olds. This is the curated version.

The format borrows from my district's instructional design work. Chunk-Chew-Check is the same scaffolding I use with students; it turns out it works on adults reading dense developmental psychology for the same reasons it works on kids reading a primary source. The voice is teacher-to-teacher, and parent-to-parent. Not preachy, not corporate, not "edutainment." If a section feels like a PD slide deck, that's a bug, not a feature.

Every link in the library and every cited episode in the modules has been verified. If a link breaks, the speaker and episode title should be enough to find the new home through a quick search. If something feels off or you spot an error, please tell me. This is a working document, not a finished one.

Start with Module I if you want the foundation. Skip to Module II if you want sentences you can use tomorrow. Or open The Library if you'd rather browse the source material on your own.

Practice

When this happens, try this.

A small set of scenarios you'll actually walk into, with the specific move worth trying. Each one points back to the module step where the move is built out, so you can read the deeper version when you have time.

If your teen blows up at you over something small
Try acknowledging the feeling before anything else.

The move is not to solve the small thing. It is to name the size of the reaction. It sounds like you've had a really hard day beats This is not worth being upset about almost every time. Faber's four-step acknowledgment is the script; Damour's framing of intensity as information is the reason it works.

Module II · Listening Before Talking
If your son has gone quiet and stays quiet
Try talking sideways instead of head-on.

Natterson's most reliable move is to put the conversation in motion. Drive somewhere together. Walk the dog together. Do dishes side by side. No eye contact, no fixed agenda. The words sneak in when nothing else is happening. Boys especially seem to find the angle of approach more important than the words you choose.

Module III · Silence, Shutdown, and Boys
If you're about to ground them for the month
Pause. Reach for an alternative first.

Punishment rarely produces the change you actually want. Faber and Mazlish offer five alternatives that do: state your feelings, state your expectations, show how to make amends, offer a choice, take action. Damour reframes it the same way in audio form. The pause is the move. The alternative comes after the pause.

Module V · Conflict, Limits, and Repair
If your kid seems to care more about their friends than about you
Don't compete. Be the relationship that doesn't change.

This shift is developmental, not personal. Neufeld and Maté make the case (a polemical one, worth pushing back on) that the gap between peer-orientation and adult attachment has widened in the last generation. Whether you fully buy the thesis or not, the practical move is the same: keep showing up in small, regular ways. A meal. A walk. Ten minutes at bedtime. The relationship grows in those.

Module IV · Friendship and Belonging
If you blew it during a fight with them
Go back. The repair is the move that builds the relationship.

Siegel calls this returning home. Name what happened, take responsibility for the part that was yours, and ask what would help. No but. No relitigating the original conflict. The rupture isn't the problem; the absence of repair is. Most parents can manage the repair sentence. Most parents never quite get around to it.

Module V · Conflict, Limits, and Repair
If you can't tell whether they're struggling or in real trouble
Use Damour's three-question diagnostic.

Ask yourself: is the level of distress proportionate to what's happened? Does it pass on its own? Is the kid still functioning in the rest of life? If yes to all three, you're likely looking at ordinary distress, and your job is steady scaffolding. If no to any of them, it's time to bring in a professional. The test is a lot more useful than waiting for the situation to declare itself.

Module I · The Adolescent Brain Module III · Silence, Shutdown, and Boys
When a kid is monosyllabic at their desk
Don't demand speech. Make small invitations they can refuse.

A passing comment with no follow-up does more work than a check-in conversation. Glad you're here today. Then move on. Damour's chapter on regaining emotional control distinguishes healthy quiet (a kid regulating) from harmful quiet (a kid sealing off). Most classroom silence is the first kind. The work is to keep the door cracked, not to pry it open.

Module III · Silence, Shutdown, and Boys
When a kid is seeking attention disruptively
Name the feeling once, quietly. Then move on.

The disruption is usually about belonging, not authority. A brief acknowledgment defuses faster than escalation. I can see you're frustrated. Let's talk after class. The move borrows Faber's first acknowledgment step and Fagell's instinct to translate raw behavior into something nameable. After class, the conversation might be a real one.

Module II · Listening Before Talking Module IV · Friendship and Belonging
When a student keeps not turning in homework
Run Faber's five-step problem-solving instead of escalating consequences.

Bring it up calmly. Listen to their side first, before anything else. Share your view. Brainstorm together. Write down what you both agreed to. The five steps reliably outperform punitive consequences because the kid leaves with a plan they helped build, not a punishment they're stewing about. The first time you run it cold, it feels awkward. The second time it doesn't.

Module V · Conflict, Limits, and Repair
When a kid melts down in class
Drop the lesson for thirty seconds. Acknowledge before redirecting.

The first words out of your mouth are not calm down and not everyone, eyes back to the board. They are something close to I can see this is really hard right now. Take a minute. The thirty seconds you spend doing this saves the next ten you'd otherwise spend pulling the room back together. Damour's framing of distress as information is what makes the pause feel sane.

Module II · Listening Before Talking
When a student stops trying
Ask what's underneath. Not why they aren't applying themselves.

Disengagement is almost never about the work. It's a friendship rupture, a family situation, an undiagnosed learning difficulty, or one specific class period that's gone bad. Fagell's chapter on intervening when school is a struggle gives you a working set of what to say moves. Try the curious-not-frustrated version: I've noticed you've been quiet in here lately. Is there something I should know?

Module VI · What Students Actually Want from Us Module IV · Friendship and Belonging
When two friends are fighting and one is in your classroom
Be curious. Don't arbitrate.

You don't have to resolve the rift. You don't have power over the other half of it anyway. Help the kid in front of you name what's happening, decide what they want to do next, and rehearse the actual sentence they could use. Fagell's Chapter 6 is built around this exact move. The kid usually doesn't need a solution from you; they need help locating their own.

Module IV · Friendship and Belonging

These twelve are not the whole repertoire. They're the ones I reach for most. The modules are where the work is built out; this page is a shortcut for the situation you're standing in right now.

The Library

Seven books, paired with their authors' own voices

Each book on the path is paired here with a verified audio or video companion — usually an interview with the author. Read first or listen first, whichever fits your week.

§ The Pairings

Read and listen, in any order

No. I

Brainstorm

Daniel J. Siegel

The single best book on what's actually happening inside the adolescent brain, written by a neuropsychiatrist who refuses to call any of it pathology. His ESSENCE framework — Emotional spark, Social engagement, Novelty-seeking, Creative exploration — reframes everything you thought was a problem as a feature.

Read these first The introduction and the chapters laying out the ESSENCE framework. Skim later chapters on attachment unless they speak to a specific question.
Listen / Watch Brainstorm — Dan Siegel at Talks at Google (March 2014) A full hour of Siegel walking through ESSENCE in his own words. The best companion if the book is too dense or if you'd rather start by listening on a commute. Also: Frances Jensen on NPR Fresh Air, "Why Teens Are Impulsive, Addiction-Prone And Should Protect Their Brains" — the neurology angle with a transcript.
Foundations Brain Module I Module V
No. I
The cheat sheet
Try saying
  • Your behavior makes sense to you. Help me see what I'm missing.
  • I notice you're pulled by what feels new and exciting. That's not a problem.
  • I'm not asking you to be safe. I'm asking you to be aware.
In practice
  • Reframe risk-taking as hyperrational: teens weight reward higher, not consequence lower.
  • Name the four ESSENCE traits when you see them in action: emotional spark, social engagement, novelty-seeking, creative exploration.
  • Treat what looks like “immaturity” as developmental work doing exactly what it's supposed to.
Tap to flip back
No. II

The Emotional Lives of Teenagers

Lisa Damour

Damour is the clearest contemporary voice on adolescent development — calm, specific, and unwilling to traffic in panic. This book is built around the function of emotion, why teenage feelings are intense by design, and how adults systematically misread distress.

Read these first The early chapters on what emotions are for and the chapter on the difference between mental illness and ordinary suffering. The "what to say" sections at chapter ends are the goldmine.
Listen / Watch The Emotional Lives of Teenagers — Lisa Damour on TELUS Talks Damour walking through the book's central arguments in interview form. Good first listen before diving in. Also: The Social Work Podcast Episodes 134 & 135 — a two-part deeper interview. And Damour's ongoing Ask Lisa podcast (with Reena Ninan) covers many of these themes weekly.
Communication Emotion Module I Module II
No. II
The cheat sheet
Try saying
  • You're really upset about this, aren't you?
  • Tell me more.
  • That sounds awful.
  • Do you want me to listen, or do you want me to help think it through?
In practice
  • Trust that emotional intensity is a sign the system is working.
  • Distinguish ordinary distress from clinical concern using her three-myth diagnostic.
  • Skip the “at least” reflex. It minimizes; it doesn't help.
Tap to flip back
No. III

How to Talk So Teens Will Listen
& Listen So Teens Will Talk

Adele Faber & Elaine Mazlish

A working manual disguised as a parenting book. Built from cartoon-paneled scripts that show the wrong way and the right way to handle a moment, side by side. Embarrassingly useful for teachers who want a different sentence to try tomorrow.

Read these first The chapter on dealing with feelings (the foundation for everything else), and the chapter on engaging cooperation. The problem-solving chapter is the deepest skill in the book.
Listen Adele Faber interview on The Psych Files podcast (two parts) Faber herself walking through the philosophy behind the work. Adele Faber is in her 90s and Elaine Mazlish has passed, so newer audio with the original authors is rare — this Psych Files interview is the strongest pairing. Also: Joanna Faber (Adele's daughter) and Julie King carry the work forward in How to Talk When Kids Won't Listen with active podcast appearances — search either name plus "podcast."
Communication Scripts Module II Module V
No. III
The cheat sheet
Try saying
  • Oh.
  • Mmm.
  • I see.
  • That sounds really frustrating.
  • I wish I could give you that.
In practice
  • Use the four moves in order: full attention, a sound, name the feeling, give in fantasy.
  • Describe what you see instead of giving orders (“There's wet laundry on the floor”).
  • Offer a choice instead of issuing a command (“Hang it up now or after dinner?”).
  • Reach for problem-solving before reaching for punishment.
Tap to flip back
No. IV

Middle School Matters

Phyllis L. Fagell

Written by a school counselor who has sat across from a thousand twelve-year-olds. Organized around ten skills middle schoolers need to develop, each chapter paired with specific scenarios and conversation starters. The closest thing this library has to a teacher's handbook.

Read these first The chapters on friendship navigation and self-advocacy. The chapter on asking for help is unexpectedly useful for any teacher wondering why kids won't come to office hours.
Listen Psychologists Off the Clock Ep. 272 — Middle School Matters with Phyllis Fagell A focused hour on the book's central skills with a thoughtful host. Probably the best single listen for teachers. Also: Fagell co-hosts the Middle School Walk & Talk podcast with Principal Joe Mazza for the Association for Middle Level Education (AMLE) — ongoing teacher-focused episodes.
Friendship Classroom Module IV Module VI
No. IV
The cheat sheet
Try saying
  • Is there anything you wish I knew?
  • What went well today, and what didn't?
  • Do you think this is harder for you than for other kids?
  • I've noticed…
In practice
  • Treat middle school as their developmental stage, not a junior version of high school.
  • Ask sideways, not head-on. Best conversations happen in the car or on a walk.
  • Use the “what to say” sidebars in each chapter as scripts you can lift directly.
  • Build one of the ten skills at a time. Don't try to fix everything at once.
Tap to flip back
No. V

Decoding Boys

Cara Natterson

Natterson, a pediatrician, takes on the question most parenting books duck: what to do when a boy goes quiet. The science of pubertal timing, the cultural pressure to withdraw, and the case for staying close without forcing eye contact.

Read these first The chapter on silence and the chapter on the timing of puberty. Skip ahead to her communication suggestions if you're short on time.
Listen / Watch On Boys Podcast — Decoding Boys with Dr. Cara Natterson The most popular On Boys episode of 2020 — Natterson on the central thesis of the book in conversation. Roughly 45 minutes. Also: Natterson co-hosts The Puberty Podcast with Vanessa Kroll Bennett (ongoing); and Mindful Mama Mentor Ep. 570 features Natterson on boys specifically.
Boys Silence Module III
No. V
The cheat sheet
Try saying
  • Start talking. Take any discomfort you feel and multiply by 100, then talk anyway.
  • Listen. When he's ready, listen more than you advise.
  • Don't make eye contact. Talk while driving, while walking, while doing dishes.
  • Turn off the phones. Yours and his.
In practice
  • Use the car. Side-by-side beats face-to-face for boys.
  • Assume puberty has started, even if he still looks like a kid.
  • Don't lecture. Ask, listen, then keep coming back.
  • Read Chapter 1 and treat its ten rules as a list to memorize.
Tap to flip back
No. VI

Fires in the Middle School Bathroom

Kathleen Cushman & Laura Rogers

Built almost entirely from interviews with actual middle schoolers, this is the rare book that hands the microphone back to the students. They tell you, in their own words, what makes a teacher worth listening to and what destroys their trust. Read this last if you want to test every other book against the only audience that matters.

Read these first The chapters on teacher-student relationships and on what kids want when they're struggling. Most chapters can be skimmed in 20 minutes thanks to the interview-driven structure.
No author interview verified Cushman's nonprofit What Kids Can Do hosts archived student writing in the spirit of the book. As a thematic companion, search Edutopia for "student voice" — the same orientation in video form.
Student Voice Classroom Module VI
No. VI
The cheat sheet
Try saying
  • An ideal teacher understands and pays attention to the kid.
  • Don't single one of us out.
  • Tell us why it matters.
  • Make it hard, and show us how to do it.
In practice
  • Take the student quotes literally. They are data, not opinions.
  • Combine clear expectations with warmth. Kids report wanting both.
  • Ask, once a quarter: what should I keep doing, stop doing, start doing?
  • When you read this book, read it last. Use it to test every other book.
Tap to flip back
No. VII

Hold On to Your Kids

Gordon Neufeld & Gabor Maté

The most polemical book in the library. Argues that the central problem of modern adolescence is "peer orientation" — kids attaching primarily to other kids rather than to adults — and that nearly every other symptom flows from that. You don't have to agree with all of it. You will not read it and think about the lunchroom the same way again.

Read these first The opening chapters that lay out the peer-orientation thesis. The middle chapters on attachment are dense but pay off. The prescriptive chapters at the end are where opinions diverge most.
Listen What Fresh Hell — Dr. Gabor Maté and Dr. Gordon Neufeld together on Maintaining Healthy Connection Rare to get both authors in the same conversation. They walk through the peer-orientation thesis from the revised edition. The single best companion to the book. Also: Mindful Mama Mentor Ep. 481 features Maté alone on the book; the Neufeld Institute (Vancouver) hosts Neufeld's own talks on attachment-based parenting.
Attachment Theory Module IV
No. VII
The cheat sheet
Try saying
  • What would you like to do together this week?
  • Tell me about your day before I tell you about mine.
  • I'm here when you're ready.
In practice
  • Notice when your kid is orienting toward peers more than adults.
  • Collect your kid before correcting them. Connection before instruction.
  • Build small daily rituals: a meal, a walk, ten minutes at bedtime.
  • Don't compete with peers. Be the relationship that doesn't change.
